I'm also actively avoiding the freeway, even rearranging my work schedule to do so, and I recently had to leave a community festival early because hearing distant sirens overwhelmed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulty recalling important aspects of the crash itself, or do you find that you're remembering it all too vividly?\n\nPatient: It’s actually a mix of both. I get these sudden, vivid flashes during the day where I see the driver's bloody face and hear the metal crunching, but at the same time, there are huge blanks in my memory from that night, like not remembering what I said on the 911 call or how the fire trucks even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of being constantly on edge and scanning for danger are significantly triggered or worsened by specific stimuli, such as loud noises, crowded areas, or certain smells that remind you of the crash?"

Yes, absolutely. Just hearing sirens in the distance, like at that community festival, completely overwhelms me and makes me feel like I have to escape. screeching brakes and can’t get back to sleep, so I drag through work like I never really rested.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere during the day, like in a meeting or standing in line for coffee, I’ll suddenly see that guy’s bloody face and hear the crunch of metal as clear as if I’m back on the shoulder, and my heart just takes off and I lose track of what I was doing.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): What’s weird is there are big chunks of that night I just can’t pull up—like I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I honestly don’t remember exactly what I said or even how the fire trucks got there, it’s just a blank between a few sharp moments.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Even when my kid’s team won their soccer game and everyone was cheering, I was just standing there feeling flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on TV instead of being there, and I couldn’t tap into any real joy about it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’ve been snapping at people over nothing—snarling at my wife because she asked if I was okay, and barking at my staff for small mistakes in the safety reports—then feeling stupid about it a few minutes later but not sure how to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Whether I’m driving on side streets or just walking around that festival, my eyes are constantly flicking to every car, exit, and siren, my shoulders are tight like I’m bracing for another impact, and I can’t seem to switch that off long enough to actually relax.\n▸ Additional socially I’ve bailed early on things like that community festival because I feel too overwhelmed to stay and actually participate.\n- duration: All of this—being on edge, the bad sleep, the flashes of A close friend finally convinced me to come in because it's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any recurring, distressing memories or flashbacks of the freeway crash, or do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yes, both of those things are happening. During the day, I'll suddenly see that injured driver's bloody face and hear the crunch of metal, which makes my heart race. I'm also actively avoiding the freeway, even rearranging my work schedule to do so, and I recently had to leave a community festival early because hearing distant sirens overwhelmed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulty recalling important aspects of the crash itself, or do you find that you're remembering it all too vividly?\n\nPatient: It’s actually a mix of both. I get these sudden, vivid flashes during the day where I see the driver's bloody face and hear the metal crunching, but at the same time, there are huge blanks in my memory from that night, like not remembering what I said on the 911 call or how the fire trucks even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of being constantly on edge and scanning for danger are significantly triggered or worsened by specific stimuli, such as loud noises, crowded areas, or certain smells that remind you of the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Just hearing sirens in the distance, like at that community festival, completely overwhelms me and makes me feel like I have to escape. And whenever I'm driving or around heavy traffic, I'm on high alert, constantly scanning every car and exit with my shoulders completely tensed up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you react when you're startled, for example, by a loud noise, and do you find yourself becoming easily irritated or angry, even in situations that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been incredibly irritable, snapping at my wife just for asking if I'm okay and barking at my staff over minor safety report mistakes, which makes me feel really stupid and guilty afterward. As for being startled, my body feels constantly tense and braced for impact, so even sudden things like distant sirens make me feel completely overwhelmed and highly on edge.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your sleep has been since the crash, and have you been having any nightmares related to the accident?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been terrible since the crash, as I either lie awake for hours replaying it or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from half-dreams of screeching brakes. Once I wake up, I can't get back to sleep at all. It's left me dragging through my workdays feeling like I haven't rested a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been avoiding social interactions or activities you used to enjoy because they remind you of the crash or make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I've been bailing on social plans because I feel too overwhelmed to participate, like when I had to leave that community festival early. Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.

Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there's been a significant change in how you view yourself, others, or the world since the crash, such as feeling like you're to blame for what happened or that the world is a much more dangerous place?\n\nPatient: I don't feel to blame for the accident since I just happened to be driving by, and I haven't really thought about a shift in how I view myself or others. However, I definitely feel constantly on guard now, like I'm expecting another crash to happen at any moment."

Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there's been a significant change in how you view yourself, others, or the world since the crash, such as feeling like you're to blame for what happened or that the world is a much more dangerous place?\n\nPatient: I don't feel to blame for the accident since I just happened to be driving by, and I haven't really thought about a shift in how I view myself or others. However, I definitely feel constantly on guard now, like I'm expecting another crash to happen at any moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience these intense feelings of being on edge or startled, and are they interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being on edge is constant, and I just can't seem to switch it off, especially when driving or out in public. screeching brakes and can’t get back to sleep, so I drag through work like I never really rested.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere during the day, like in a meeting or standing in line for cheering, I was just standing there feeling flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on TV instead of being there, and I couldn’t tap into any real joy about it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’ve been snapping at people over nothing—snarling at my wife because she asked if I was okay, and barking at my staff for small mistakes in the safety reports—then feeling stupid about it a few minutes later but not sure how to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Whether I’m driving on side streets or just walking around that festival, my eyes are constantly flicking to every car, exit, and siren, my shoulders are tight like I’m bracing for another impact, and I can’t seem to switch that off long enough to actually relax.\n▸ Additional     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Since that freeway crash about three weeks ago, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving. A close friend finally convinced me to come in because it's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any recurring, distressing memories or flashbacks of the freeway crash, or do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yes, both of those things are happening. During the day, I'll suddenly see that injured driver's bloody face and hear the crunch of metal, which makes my heart race. I'm also actively avoiding the freeway, even rearranging my work schedule to do so, and I recently had to leave a community festival early because hearing distant sirens overwhelmed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulty recalling important aspects of the crash itself, or do you find that you're remembering it all too vividly?\n\nPatient: It’s actually a mix of both. I get these sudden, vivid flashes during the day where I see the driver's bloody face and hear the metal crunching, but at the same time, there are huge blanks in my memory from that night, like not remembering what I said on the 911 call or how the fire trucks even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of being constantly on edge and scanning for danger are significantly triggered or worsened by specific stimuli, such as loud noises, crowded areas, or certain smells that remind you of the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Just hearing sirens in the distance, like at that community festival, completely overwhelms me and makes me feel like I have to escape. And whenever I'm driving or around heavy traffic, I'm on high alert, constantly scanning every car and exit with my shoulders completely tensed up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you react when you're startled, for example, by a loud noise, and do you find yourself becoming easily irritated or angry, even in situations that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been incredibly irritable, snapping at my wife just for asking if I'm okay and barking at my staff over minor safety report mistakes, which makes me feel really stupid and guilty afterward. As for being startled, my body feels constantly tense and braced for impact, so even sudden things like distant sirens make me feel completely overwhelmed and highly on edge.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your sleep has been since the crash, and have you been having any nightmares related to the accident?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been terrible since the crash, as I either lie awake for hours replaying it or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from half-dreams of screeching brakes. Once I wake up, I can't get back to sleep at all. It's left me dragging through my workdays feeling like I haven't rested a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been avoiding social interactions or activities you used to enjoy because they remind you of the crash or make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been bailing on social plans because I feel too overwhelmed to participate, like when I had to leave that community festival early. Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there's been a significant change in how you view yourself, others, or the world since the crash, such as feeling like you're to blame for what happened or that the world is a much more dangerous place?\n\nPatient: I don't feel to blame for the accident since I just happened to be driving by, and I haven't really thought about a shift in how I view myself or others. However, I definitely feel constantly on guard now, like I'm expecting another crash to happen at any moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience these intense feelings of being on edge or startled, and are they interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being on edge is constant, and I just can't seem to switch it off, especially when driving or out in public. It's really interfering with my life because I've had to rearrange my whole work schedule to avoid freeways, I'm zoning out in safety meetings, and I keep snapping at my wife and staff over the smallest things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having huge blanks in your memory from that night, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'huge blanks' and how often you experience these gaps in memory?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I'm forgetting new things now, but there are just specific gaps from the night of the crash itself. I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I have no memory of what I actually said or how the fire trucks even arrived. It’s just a strange, empty blank space between a few very sharp, vivid moments.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by 'a strange, empty blank space' in your memory, and do you feel like you're disconnected from your body or the world around you during these episodes or at any other times since the crash?"
  }

The blank spaces are just parts of that night that are completely gone, like a tape that was cut between the crash and the first responders arriving. As for feeling disconnected, I often feel detached from the world, like I'm watching my life on a TV screen instead of being present, even when I'm at my kid's soccer games.

Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Ever since the crash, I either lie there for hours replaying it in my head or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from these half‑dreams of (Irritability_Aggression): I’ve been snapping at people over nothing—snarling at my wife because she asked if I was okay, and barking at my staff for small mistakes in the safety reports—then feeling stupid about it a few minutes later but not sure how to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Whether I’m driving on side streets or just walking around that festival, my eyes are constantly flicking to every car, exit, and siren, my shoulders are tight like I’m bracing for another impact, and I can’t seem to switch that off long enough to actually relax.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last few weeks I’ve had to shuffle my whole work schedule to avoid freeway drives and I catch myself zoning out in safety meetings, and socially I’ve bailed early on things like that community festival because I feel too overwhelmed to stay and actually participate.\n- duration: All of this—being on edge, the bad sleep, the flashes of the crash—has been going on ever since that freeway pileup about three weeks ago.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Since that freeway crash about three weeks ago, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving. A close friend finally convinced me to come in because it's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any recurring, distressing memories or flashbacks of the freeway crash, or do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yes, both of those things are happening. During the day, I'll suddenly see that injured driver's bloody face and hear the crunch of metal, which makes my heart race. I'm also actively avoiding the freeway, even rearranging my work schedule to do so, and I recently had to leave a community festival early because hearing distant sirens overwhelmed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulty recalling important aspects of the crash itself, or do you find that you're remembering it all too vividly?\n\nPatient: It’s actually a mix of both. I get these sudden, vivid flashes during the day where I see the driver's bloody face and hear the metal crunching, but at the same time, there are huge blanks in my memory from that night, like not remembering what I said on the 911 call or how the fire trucks even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of being constantly on edge and scanning for danger are significantly triggered or worsened by specific stimuli, such as loud noises, crowded areas, or certain smells that remind you of the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Just hearing sirens in the distance, like at that community festival, completely overwhelms me and makes me feel like I have to escape. And whenever I'm driving or around heavy traffic, I'm on high alert, constantly scanning every car and exit with my shoulders completely tensed up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you react when you're startled, for example, by a loud noise, and do you find yourself becoming easily irritated or angry, even in situations that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been incredibly irritable, snapping at my wife just for asking if I'm okay and barking at my staff over minor safety report mistakes, which makes me feel really stupid and guilty afterward. As for being startled, my body feels constantly tense and braced for impact, so even sudden things like distant sirens make me feel completely overwhelmed and highly on edge.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your sleep has been since the crash, and have you been having any nightmares related to the accident?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been terrible since the crash, as I either lie awake for hours replaying it or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from half-dreams of screeching brakes. Once I wake up, I can't get back to sleep at all. It's left me dragging through my workdays feeling like I haven't rested a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been avoiding social interactions or activities you used to enjoy because they remind you of the crash or make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been bailing on social plans because I feel too overwhelmed to participate, like when I had to leave that community festival early. Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there's been a significant change in how you view yourself, others, or the world since the crash, such as feeling like you're to blame for what happened or that the world is a much more dangerous place?\n\nPatient: I don't feel to blame for the accident since I just happened to be driving by, and I haven't really thought about a shift in how I view myself or others. However, I definitely feel constantly on guard now, like I'm expecting another crash to happen at any moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience these intense feelings of being on edge or startled, and are they interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being on edge is constant, and I just can't seem to switch it off, especially when driving or out in public. It's really interfering with my life because I've had to rearrange my whole work schedule to avoid freeways, I'm zoning out in safety meetings, and I keep snapping at my wife and staff over the smallest things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having huge blanks in your memory from that night, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'huge blanks' and how often you experience these gaps in memory?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I'm forgetting new things now, but there are just specific gaps from the night of the crash itself. I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I have no memory of what I actually said or how the fire trucks even arrived. It’s just a strange, empty blank space between a few very sharp, vivid moments.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by 'a strange, empty blank space' in your memory, and do you feel like you're disconnected from your body or the world around you during these episodes or at any other times since the crash?\n\nPatient: The blank spaces are just parts of that night that are completely gone, like a tape that was cut between the crash and the first responders arriving. As for feeling disconnected, I often feel detached from the world, like I'm watching my life on a TV screen instead of being present, even when I'm at my kid's soccer games.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often you feel like you're watching your life on a TV screen, and does this feeling of detachment happen only during specific situations or is it a more constant experience for you since the crash?"

It's incredibly frustrating because I just haven't been able to tap into any real joy since the crash.

He spent nearly an hour on the shoulder coordinating with 911, directing traffic away from the wreckage, and watching first responders extract injured drivers, one of whom he had briefly spoken to while trying to keep them conscious. Over the next three weeks, he forced himself to keep up his operations manager role and weekend soccer commitments, but colleagues noticed he was unusually distracted in safety meetings and started rearranging his schedule to avoid driving on the freeway, which complicated his commute and site visits. When he finally had to leave a community festival early because he felt overwhelmed just hearing sirens in the distance, a close friend urged him to see a clinician through his primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: “Since that freeway crash, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Ever since the crash, I either lie there for hours replaying it in my head or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from these half‑dreams of screeching brakes and can’t get back to sleep, so I drag through work like I never really rested.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere during the day, like in a meeting or standing in line for coffee, I’ll suddenly see that guy’s bloody face and hear the crunch of metal as clear as if I’m back on the shoulder, and my heart just takes off and I lose track of what I was doing.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): What’s weird is there are big chunks of that night I just can’t pull up—like I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I honestly don’t remember exactly what I said or even how the fire trucks got there, it’s just a blank between a few sharp moments.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Even when my kid’s team won their soccer game and everyone was cheering, I was just standing there feeling flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on TV instead of being there, and I couldn’t tap into any real joy about it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’ve been snapping at people over nothing—snarling at my wife because she asked if I was okay, and barking at my staff for small mistakes in the safety reports—then feeling stupid about it a few minutes later but not sure how to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Whether I’m driving on side streets or just walking around that festival, my eyes are constantly flicking to every car, exit, and siren, my shoulders are tight like I’m bracing for another impact, and I can’t seem to switch that off long enough to actually relax.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last few weeks I’ve had to shuffle my whole work schedule to avoid freeway drives and I catch myself zoning out in safety meetings, and socially I’ve bailed early on things like that community festival because I feel too overwhelmed to stay and actually participate.\n- duration: All of this—being on edge, the bad sleep, the flashes of the crash—has been going on ever since that freeway pileup about three weeks ago.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Since that freeway crash about three weeks ago, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving. A close friend finally convinced me to come in because it's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any recurring, distressing memories or flashbacks of the freeway crash, or do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yes, both of those things are happening. During the day, I'll suddenly see that injured driver's bloody face and hear the crunch of metal, which makes my heart race. I'm also actively avoiding the freeway, even rearranging my work schedule to do so, and I recently had to leave a community festival early because hearing distant sirens overwhelmed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulty recalling important aspects of the crash itself, or do you find that you're remembering it all too vividly?\n\nPatient: It’s actually a mix of both. I get these sudden, vivid flashes during the day where I see the driver's bloody face and hear the metal crunching, but at the same time, there are huge blanks in my memory from that night, like not remembering what I said on the 911 call or how the fire trucks even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of being constantly on edge and scanning for danger are significantly triggered or worsened by specific stimuli, such as loud noises, crowded areas, or certain smells that remind you of the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Just hearing sirens in the distance, like at that community festival, completely overwhelms me and makes me feel like I have to escape. And whenever I'm driving or around heavy traffic, I'm on high alert, constantly scanning every car and exit with my shoulders completely tensed up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you react when you're startled, for example, by a loud noise, and do you find yourself becoming easily irritated or angry, even in situations that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been incredibly irritable, snapping at my wife just for asking if I'm okay and barking at my staff over minor safety report mistakes, which makes me feel really stupid and guilty afterward. As for being startled, my body feels constantly tense and braced for impact, so even sudden things like distant sirens make me feel completely overwhelmed and highly on edge.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your sleep has been since the crash, and have you been having any nightmares related to the accident?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been terrible since the crash, as I either lie awake for hours replaying it or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from half-dreams of screeching brakes. Once I wake up, I can't get back to sleep at all. It's left me dragging through my workdays feeling like I haven't rested a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been avoiding social interactions or activities you used to enjoy because they remind you of the crash or make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been bailing on social plans because I feel too overwhelmed to participate, like when I had to leave that community festival early. Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there's been a significant change in how you view yourself, others, or the world since the crash, such as feeling like you're to blame for what happened or that the world is a much more dangerous place?\n\nPatient: I don't feel to blame for the accident since I just happened to be driving by, and I haven't really thought about a shift in how I view myself or others. However, I definitely feel constantly on guard now, like I'm expecting another crash to happen at any moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience these intense feelings of being on edge or startled, and are they interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being on edge is constant, and I just can't seem to switch it off, especially when driving or out in public. It's really interfering with my life because I've had to rearrange my whole work schedule to avoid freeways, I'm zoning out in safety meetings, and I keep snapping at my wife and staff over the smallest things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having huge blanks in your memory from that night, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'huge blanks' and how often you experience these gaps in memory?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I'm forgetting new things now, but there are just specific gaps from the night of the crash itself. I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I have no memory of what I actually said or how the fire trucks even arrived. It’s just a strange, empty blank space between a few very sharp, vivid moments.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by 'a strange, empty blank space' in your memory, and do you feel like you're disconnected from your body or the world around you during these episodes or at any other times since the crash?\n\nPatient: The blank spaces are just parts of that night that are completely gone, like a tape that was cut between the crash and the first responders arriving. As for feeling disconnected, I often feel detached from the world, like I'm watching my life on a TV screen instead of being present, even when I'm at my kid's soccer games.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often you feel like you're watching your life on a TV screen, and does this feeling of detachment happen only during specific situations or is it a more constant experience for you since the crash?\n\nPatient: It’s not constant, but it really hits me during moments when I should be feeling happy or engaged, like at my kid's soccer games. Even when they won and everyone was cheering, I just stood there feeling completely flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on a TV screen instead of actually being there. It's incredibly frustrating because I just haven't been able to tap into any real joy since the crash.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days out of the past week you've felt this way, like you're watching your life from outside your body, and has it been getting better, worse, or staying about the same?"
  }

I’d say I feel that disconnected, TV-screen feeling most days of the week, especially when I'm trying to be there for my family or engage with my team at work. It hasn't really gotten any better over these past three weeks; it has just stayed consistently flat and frustrating.

He spent nearly an hour on the shoulder coordinating with 911, directing traffic away from the wreckage, and watching first responders extract injured drivers, one of whom he had briefly spoken to while trying to keep them conscious. Over the next three weeks, he forced himself to keep up his operations manager role and weekend soccer commitments, but colleagues noticed he was unusually distracted in safety meetings and started rearranging his schedule to avoid driving on the freeway, which complicated his commute and site visits. When he finally had to leave a community festival early because he felt overwhelmed just hearing sirens in the distance, a close friend urged him to see a clinician through his primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: “Since that freeway crash, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Ever since the crash, I either lie there for hours replaying it in my head or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from these half‑dreams of screeching brakes and can’t get back to sleep, so I drag through work like I never really rested.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere during the day, like in a meeting or standing in line for coffee, I’ll suddenly see that guy’s bloody face and hear the crunch of metal as clear as if I’m back on the shoulder, and my heart just takes off and I lose track of what I was doing.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): What’s weird is there are big chunks of that night I just can’t pull up—like I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I honestly don’t remember exactly what I said or even how the fire trucks got there, it’s just a blank between a few sharp moments.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Even when my kid’s team won their soccer game and everyone was cheering, I was just standing there feeling flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on TV instead of being there, and I couldn’t tap into any real joy about it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’ve been snapping at people over nothing—snarling at my wife because she asked if I was okay, and barking at my staff for small mistakes in the safety reports—then feeling stupid about it a few minutes later but not sure how to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Whether I’m driving on side streets or just walking around that festival, my eyes are constantly flicking to every car, exit, and siren, my shoulders are tight like I’m bracing for another impact, and I can’t seem to switch that off long enough to actually relax.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last few weeks I’ve had to shuffle my whole work schedule to avoid freeway drives and I catch myself zoning out in safety meetings, and socially I’ve bailed early on things like that community festival because I feel too overwhelmed to stay and actually participate.\n- duration: All of this—being on edge, the bad sleep, the flashes of the crash—has been going on ever since that freeway pileup about three weeks ago.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Since that freeway crash about three weeks ago, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving. A close friend finally convinced me to come in because it's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any recurring, distressing memories or flashbacks of the freeway crash, or do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yes, both of those things are happening. During the day, I'll suddenly see that injured driver's bloody face and hear the crunch of metal, which makes my heart race. I'm also actively avoiding the freeway, even rearranging my work schedule to do so, and I recently had to leave a community festival early because hearing distant sirens overwhelmed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulty recalling important aspects of the crash itself, or do you find that you're remembering it all too vividly?\n\nPatient: It’s actually a mix of both. I get these sudden, vivid flashes during the day where I see the driver's bloody face and hear the metal crunching, but at the same time, there are huge blanks in my memory from that night, like not remembering what I said on the 911 call or how the fire trucks even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of being constantly on edge and scanning for danger are significantly triggered or worsened by specific stimuli, such as loud noises, crowded areas, or certain smells that remind you of the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Just hearing sirens in the distance, like at that community festival, completely overwhelms me and makes me feel like I have to escape. And whenever I'm driving or around heavy traffic, I'm on high alert, constantly scanning every car and exit with my shoulders completely tensed up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you react when you're startled, for example, by a loud noise, and do you find yourself becoming easily irritated or angry, even in situations that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been incredibly irritable, snapping at my wife just for asking if I'm okay and barking at my staff over minor safety report mistakes, which makes me feel really stupid and guilty afterward. As for being startled, my body feels constantly tense and braced for impact, so even sudden things like distant sirens make me feel completely overwhelmed and highly on edge.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your sleep has been since the crash, and have you been having any nightmares related to the accident?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been terrible since the crash, as I either lie awake for hours replaying it or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from half-dreams of screeching brakes. Once I wake up, I can't get back to sleep at all. It's left me dragging through my workdays feeling like I haven't rested a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been avoiding social interactions or activities you used to enjoy because they remind you of the crash or make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been bailing on social plans because I feel too overwhelmed to participate, like when I had to leave that community festival early. Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there's been a significant change in how you view yourself, others, or the world since the crash, such as feeling like you're to blame for what happened or that the world is a much more dangerous place?\n\nPatient: I don't feel to blame for the accident since I just happened to be driving by, and I haven't really thought about a shift in how I view myself or others. However, I definitely feel constantly on guard now, like I'm expecting another crash to happen at any moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience these intense feelings of being on edge or startled, and are they interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being on edge is constant, and I just can't seem to switch it off, especially when driving or out in public. It's really interfering with my life because I've had to rearrange my whole work schedule to avoid freeways, I'm zoning out in safety meetings, and I keep snapping at my wife and staff over the smallest things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having huge blanks in your memory from that night, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'huge blanks' and how often you experience these gaps in memory?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I'm forgetting new things now, but there are just specific gaps from the night of the crash itself. I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I have no memory of what I actually said or how the fire trucks even arrived. It’s just a strange, empty blank space between a few very sharp, vivid moments.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by 'a strange, empty blank space' in your memory, and do you feel like you're disconnected from your body or the world around you during these episodes or at any other times since the crash?\n\nPatient: The blank spaces are just parts of that night that are completely gone, like a tape that was cut between the crash and the first responders arriving. As for feeling disconnected, I often feel detached from the world, like I'm watching my life on a TV screen instead of being present, even when I'm at my kid's soccer games.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often you feel like you're watching your life on a TV screen, and does this feeling of detachment happen only during specific situations or is it a more constant experience for you since the crash?\n\nPatient: It’s not constant, but it really hits me during moments when I should be feeling happy or engaged, like at my kid's soccer games. Even when they won and everyone was cheering, I just stood there feeling completely flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on a TV screen instead of actually being there. It's incredibly frustrating because I just haven't been able to tap into any real joy since the crash.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days out of the past week you've felt this way, like you're watching your life from outside your body, and has it been getting better, worse, or staying about the same?\n\nPatient: I’d say I feel that disconnected, TV-screen feeling most days of the week, especially when I'm trying to be there for my family or engage with my team at work. It hasn't really gotten any better over these past three weeks; it has just stayed consistently flat and frustrating.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms have been persistent over the past three weeks, but can you tell me if there have been any days or moments where you felt somewhat better or more like your old self, even if it was just for a brief period?"
  }
]

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Since that freeway crash about three weeks ago, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving. A close friend finally convinced me to come in because it's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any recurring, distressing memories or flashbacks of the freeway crash, or do you find yourself avoiding thoughts or reminders of it?\n\nPatient: Yes, both of those things are happening. During the day, I'll suddenly see that injured driver's bloody face and hear the crunch of metal, which makes my heart race. I'm also actively avoiding the freeway, even rearranging my work schedule to do so, and I recently had to leave a community festival early because hearing distant sirens overwhelmed me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulty recalling important aspects of the crash itself, or do you find that you're remembering it all too vividly?\n\nPatient: It’s actually a mix of both. I get these sudden, vivid flashes during the day where I see the driver's bloody face and hear the metal crunching, but at the same time, there are huge blanks in my memory from that night, like not remembering what I said on the 911 call or how the fire trucks even got there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your feelings of being constantly on edge and scanning for danger are significantly triggered or worsened by specific stimuli, such as loud noises, crowded areas, or certain smells that remind you of the crash?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Just hearing sirens in the distance, like at that community festival, completely overwhelms me and makes me feel like I have to escape. And whenever I'm driving or around heavy traffic, I'm on high alert, constantly scanning every car and exit with my shoulders completely tensed up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you react when you're startled, for example, by a loud noise, and do you find yourself becoming easily irritated or angry, even in situations that wouldn't normally bother you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been incredibly irritable, snapping at my wife just for asking if I'm okay and barking at my staff over minor safety report mistakes, which makes me feel really stupid and guilty afterward. As for being startled, my body feels constantly tense and braced for impact, so even sudden things like distant sirens make me feel completely overwhelmed and highly on edge.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your sleep has been since the crash, and have you been having any nightmares related to the accident?\n\nPatient: My sleep has been terrible since the crash, as I either lie awake for hours replaying it or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from half-dreams of screeching brakes. Once I wake up, I can't get back to sleep at all. It's left me dragging through my workdays feeling like I haven't rested a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been avoiding social interactions or activities you used to enjoy because they remind you of the crash or make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been bailing on social plans because I feel too overwhelmed to participate, like when I had to leave that community festival early. Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there's been a significant change in how you view yourself, others, or the world since the crash, such as feeling like you're to blame for what happened or that the world is a much more dangerous place?\n\nPatient: I don't feel to blame for the accident since I just happened to be driving by, and I haven't really thought about a shift in how I view myself or others. However, I definitely feel constantly on guard now, like I'm expecting another crash to happen at any moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience these intense feelings of being on edge or startled, and are they interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being on edge is constant, and I just can't seem to switch it off, especially when driving or out in public. It's really interfering with my life because I've had to rearrange my whole work schedule to avoid freeways, I'm zoning out in safety meetings, and I keep snapping at my wife and staff over the smallest things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having huge blanks in your memory from that night, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'huge blanks' and how often you experience these gaps in memory?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I'm forgetting new things now, but there are just specific gaps from the night of the crash itself. I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I have no memory of what I actually said or how the fire trucks even arrived. It’s just a strange, empty blank space between a few very sharp, vivid moments.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by 'a strange, empty blank space' in your memory, and do you feel like you're disconnected from your body or the world around you during these episodes or at any other times since the crash?\n\nPatient: The blank spaces are just parts of that night that are completely gone, like a tape that was cut between the crash and the first responders arriving. As for feeling disconnected, I often feel detached from the world, like I'm watching my life on a TV screen instead of being present, even when I'm at my kid's soccer games.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often you feel like you're watching your life on a TV screen, and does this feeling of detachment happen only during specific situations or is it a more constant experience for you since the crash?\n\nPatient: It’s not constant, but it really hits me during moments when I should be feeling happy or engaged, like at my kid's soccer games. Even when they won and everyone was cheering, I just stood there feeling completely flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on a TV screen instead of actually being there. It's incredibly frustrating because I just haven't been able to tap into any real joy since the crash.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days out of the past week you've felt this way, like you're watching your life from outside your body, and has it been getting better, worse, or staying about the same?\n\nPatient: I’d say I feel that disconnected, TV-screen feeling most days of the week, especially when I'm trying to be there for my family or engage with my team at work. It hasn't really gotten any better over these past three weeks; it has just stayed consistently flat and frustrating.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms have been persistent over the past three weeks, but can you tell me if there have been any days or moments where you felt somewhat better or more like your old self, even if it was just for a brief period?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I haven't really had any moments or days where I felt like my old self since the crash. Even during times when I should be happy or relaxed—like when my kid's soccer team won—I just felt completely flat, disconnected, and unable to switch off that constant feeling of being on edge.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if the feeling of being disconnected, like watching your life on a TV screen, is more prominent during the flashbacks or if it's a separate experience that occurs even when you're not having vivid memories of the crash?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely a separate experience, not just something that happens during the flashbacks. For example, at my kid's soccer game, I wasn't picturing the crash at all, but I still felt completely flat, disconnected, and unable to share in any of the joy. It just feels like this constant, numb barrier between me and the rest of the world.

It's left me dragging through my workdays feeling like I haven't rested a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been avoiding social interactions or activities you used to enjoy because they remind you of the crash or make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been bailing on social plans because I feel too overwhelmed to participate, like when I had to leave that community festival early. Even at my kid's soccer games, which I usually love, I just feel completely flat and disconnected from everyone, like I'm unable to feel any real joy.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there's been a significant change in how you view yourself, others, or the world since the crash, such as feeling like you're to blame for what happened or that the world is a much more dangerous place?\n\nPatient: I don't feel to blame for the accident since I just happened to be driving by, and I haven't really thought about a shift in how I view myself or others. However, I definitely feel constantly on guard now, like I'm expecting another crash to happen at any moment.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how often you experience these intense feelings of being on edge or startled, and are they interfering with your daily activities or relationships?\n\nPatient: The feeling of being on edge is constant, and I just can't seem to switch it off, especially when driving or out in public. It's really interfering with my life because I've had to rearrange my whole work schedule to avoid freeways, I'm zoning out in safety meetings, and I keep snapping at my wife and staff over the smallest things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having huge blanks in your memory from that night, can you tell me more about what you mean by 'huge blanks' and how often you experience these gaps in memory?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I'm forgetting new things now, but there are just specific gaps from the night of the crash itself. I know I called 911 and talked to the injured driver, but I have no memory of what I actually said or how the fire trucks even arrived. It’s just a strange, empty blank space between a few very sharp, vivid moments.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by 'a strange, empty blank space' in your memory, and do you feel like you're disconnected from your body or the world around you during these episodes or at any other times since the crash?\n\nPatient: The blank spaces are just parts of that night that are completely gone, like a tape that was cut between the crash and the first responders arriving. As for feeling disconnected, I often feel detached from the world, like I'm watching my life on a TV screen instead of being present, even when I'm at my kid's soccer games.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often you feel like you're watching your life on a TV screen, and does this feeling of detachment happen only during specific situations or is it a more constant experience for you since the crash?\n\nPatient: It’s not constant, but it really hits me during moments when I should be feeling happy or engaged, like at my kid's soccer games. Even when they won and everyone was cheering, I just stood there feeling completely flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on a TV screen instead of actually being there. It's incredibly frustrating because I just haven't been able to tap into any real joy since the crash.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how many days out of the past week you've felt this way, like you're watching your life from outside your body, and has it been getting better, worse, or staying about the same?\n\nPatient: I’d say I feel that disconnected, TV-screen feeling most days of the week, especially when I'm trying to be there for my family or engage with my team at work. It hasn't really gotten any better over these past three weeks; it has just stayed consistently flat and frustrating.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms have been persistent over the past three weeks, but can you tell me if there have been any days or moments where you felt somewhat better or more like your old self, even if it was just for a brief period?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I haven't really had any moments or days where I felt like my old self since the crash. Even during times when I should be happy or relaxed—like when my kid's soccer team won—I just felt completely flat, disconnected, and unable to switch off that constant feeling of being on edge.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if the feeling of being disconnected, like watching your life on a TV screen, is more prominent during the flashbacks or if it's a separate experience that occurs even when you're not having vivid memories of the crash?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a separate experience, not just something that happens during the flashbacks. For example, at my kid's soccer game, I wasn't picturing the crash at all, but I still felt completely flat, disconnected, and unable to share in any of the joy. It just feels like this constant, numb barrier between me and the rest of the world."

Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.
